**Oh my greedy tongue! Don’t you know that the name of Radha is like fresh new ambrosia and the name of Krishna like wonderful sweet and thickened milk? Add the camphor scent of your attachment and mix them togetherat every moment: then, drink, drink that alone and nothing else. (Raghunath Das Goswami,** ***Abhīṣṭa-sūcanā*** **10,** ***Stavāvalī*****)**

*yaj-jāpaḥ sakṛd eva gokula-pater ākarṣakas tat-kṣaṇād*  
*yatra premavatāṁ samasta-pūruṣārtheṣu sphuret tucchatā*  
*yan-nāmāṅkita-mantra-jāpana-paraḥ prītyā svayaṁ mādhavaḥ*  
*śrī-kṛṣṇo’pi tad adbhutaṁ sphuratu me rādheti varṇa-dvayam*

**Перевод:**

**May the two wondrous syllables of Radha’s name dance forever on my tongue; by simply whispering them, one immediately attracts Krishna, and for one who loves it, all other perfections of human life seem insignificant. Even Krishna Himself is absorbed in lovingly chanting the mantra of Her names. (Prabodhananda Saraswati,** ***Rādhā-rasa-sudhā-nidhi*****, 95)**

*kālindī-taṭa-kuñja-mandira-gato yogīndravad yat-pada-*  
*jyotir-dhyāna-paraḥ sadā japati yāṁ premāśru-pūrṇo hariḥ*  
*kenāpy adbhutam ullasad-rati-rasānandena sammohitaḥ*  
*sā rādheti sadā hṛdi sphuratu me vidyā parā dvy-akṣarā*

**Перевод:**

**In the forest bower by the banks of the Yamuna, Krishna is fixed in meditation like a yogi  on the light emanating from Radha’s toenails. Muttering Her names with eyes overflowing with tears, His body overcome with an unbelievably joyful thrill of desire. May those two syllables magical of Radha’s name always reverberate in my heart. (Prabodhananda Saraswati,** ***Rādhā-rasa-sudhā-nidhi*****, 96)**

*devānām atha bhakta-mukta-suhṛdām atyanta-dūraṁ ca yat*  
*premānanda-rasaṁ mahā-sukha-karaṁ coccāritaṁ premataḥ*  
*premṇākarṇayate japaty atha mudā gāyaty athāliṣv ayaṁ*  
*jalpaty aśru-mukho haris tad amṛtaṁ rādheti me jīvanam 97*

**Перевод:**

**The two syllables of Radharani’s name are so filled with the nectar of ecstatic love they are far out of the reach of the gods, the devotees, the liberated and their friends. Yet when uttered with love, they give the greatest joy. Lord Hari Himself listens lovingly to the chanting of Her name; He sings them joyfully in the company of Her friends, and constantly repeats them with His eyes filled with tears. Those two syllables of Radharani’s name are my life and soul. (Prabodhananda Saraswati,** ***Rādhā-rasa-sudhā-nidhi*****, 97)**

*rādhā-nāma-sudhā-rasaṁ rasayituṁ jihvāstu me vihvalā*  
*pādau tat-pada-kāṅkitāsu caratāṁ vṛndāṭavī-vīthiṣu*  
*tat-karmaiva karaḥ karotu hṛdayaṁ tasyāḥ padaṁ dhyāyatāṁ*  
*tad-bhāvotsavataḥ paraṁ bhavatu me tat-prāṇanāthe ratiḥ*

**Перевод:**

**May my tongue always be anxious to relish the ambrosial flavor of Radha’s name; may my feet always walk on Vrindavan’s forest paths where Radha has left Her footprints; may my hands always be engaged in Her work and my heart meditate on Her feet. And may I always feel love for her beloved Hari, who is always absorbed in a festive mood of love for Her. (Prabodhananda Saraswati,** ***Rādhā-rasa-sudhā-nidhi*****, 142)**

*rādhā-nāmaiva kāryaṁ hy anudina-militaṁ sādhanādhīśa-koṭīs*  
*tyājyā nīrājya rādhā-pada-kamala-sudhāṁ sat-pumarthāgra-koṭīḥ*  
*rādhā-pādābja-līlā-bhuvi jayati sadā’manda-mandāra-koṭīḥ*  
*śrī-rādhā-kiṅkarīṇāṁ luṭhati caraṇayor adbhutā siddhi-koṭīḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Chant Radharani’s holy name every day, and give up all the millions of other spiritual practices, however powerful. Simply worship the nectar of Radha’s lotus feet and abandon all the millions of other pious ambitions in life. Hundreds of millions of desire trees line the land where Radharani’s lotus feet have had their pastimes and hundreds and millions of divine perfections throw themselves at the feet of Her maidservants. (Prabodhananda Saraswati,** ***Rādhā-rasa-sudhā-nidhi*****, 144)**

## Glossary

The purpose of this glossary is to discuss various terms used in this book in depth and, in some cases, to explain why certain translations have been chosen over others. Ideally, a one-to-one correspondence of technical terms from Sanskrit and Bengali to English should be possible. Even though to some extent usage dictates a word’s meaning, even when it is not necessarily the *mot juste,* this is a difficult task that may take generations to perfect,

We have also thought long and hard about the usage of certain Sanskrit and Bengali terms. Those who have taken up the culture of devotional service have come to use many such words as a natural part of their daily language. We take this to be a positive development and see no reason to insist on translating a word into English when the original Sanskrit has been freely adopted by practicing devotees, with all its nuances and contextual precision. Indeed, this process was to some extent already underway long before the arrival of Krishna consciousness in the West, and words such as karma, dharma, avatar, etc., can be found in any English dictionary. We feel that the English-speaking devotees of Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu are simply continuing this process, whereby the English language freely adopts foreign words wherever they are considered more precise and useful than any existing English word. Furthermore, we do not think that such adopted words should be marked as foreign by italicization or the use of diacritics.

This glossary indicates which of these words are being treated as “adopted” by leaving them unitalicized and free of diacritic markings. Nevertheless, to help in pronunciation and to clarify the origin of these words, we have also given the precise diacritics for them.

I have given extended definitions for many words, in certain cases supplementing the information found in *Harināma-cintāmaṇi* by quoting Bhaktivinoda Thakur’s other books, especially *Jaiva Dharma* and *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta.*

## —a—

abhas (*ābhāsa*)*—*“A semblance, appearance or facsimile.” Used to describe something that is not quite what it is supposed to be, primarily Namabhas (q.v.) and Vaishnavabhas, but also shraddhabhas (HNC 3.56ff).

*abhidhā vṛtti*—The primary or direct meaning of a word; the dictionary definition (HNC 7 note 9)

abhidheya*—*Literally “what is to be defined.” The second of three aspects of Gaudiya Vaishnava theology: what must be done. One who understands the principles of *sambandha* (q.v.) experiences a moral imperative to act on that knowledge. This is devotional service in all its aspects, or as in HNC 3.18, *nāmānuśīlana*, “the cultivation of the Holy Name.” (ch. 2)

acharya (*ācārya*)—“Exemplary teacher, authority.” One of the terms used to denote the guru.

achintya bhedabheda (*acintya-bhedābheda*)—inconceivable oneness and difference. (HNC 7)

adhikar (*adhikāra*)*—*(1) aptitude, inclination or tendency; (2) qualification; (3) rights, ownership, entitlement; (4) authority (HNC 15). The adhikar for devotional service is explained in *Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.2.14-15.

adhikari (*adhikārī*)*—*(1) One qualified for a particular spiritual path. (2) A person authorized to act on behalf of someone else.

advaita vada (*advaita-vāda*)*—*The monistic philosophy of the jnanis, which states that reality is unitary and denies the ultimate existence of varieties, even that of the distinction between God and the individual souls. This doctrine is anathema to the Vaishnavas. Also sometimes called *kevalādvaita-vāda.*

*ājñā*—“order, permission”; The seventh of the eleven aspects of the spiritual identity. From *Jaiva Dharma*, p. 592: “Orders are of two kinds: *nitya* (regular) and *naimittika* (occasional). The merciful sakhi orders you to engage in a certain regular (*nitya*) service, which you must execute without fail whenever needed throughout the day. Sometimes, the sakhi may tell you to perform some other service according to needs that may arise at the moment. These are *naimittika* orders. These too should be carefully carried out.”

*ajñāta-sukṛti—*Unconscious or unintended meritorious acts that later bear fruit. See *sukṛti.*

*ālambana—*Literally “prop, support.” One of the fundamental ingredients for rasa; a subcategory of the *vibhāvas*. In traditional Sanskrit poetics, it refers to the persons in the play or poem who are the beloved object (*viṣaya*) and the character whose love is the principal subject of description (*āśraya*). In devotional rasa, it refers to Krishna as the object, and the devotee as subject.

*āmnāya—* Scriptural knowledge received through the proper authorized disciplic succession or *paramparā*. See HNC 7.4ff.

anartha (*anartha*)*—“*contamination, obstacle”. The term derives from *artha* “value”, i.e., a “non-value.” I have often left this word untranslated. On the fourth stage of devotional advancement, the various obstacles to spiritual progress, such as one’s past karma, other desires, etc., manifest. These are fully explained in Bhajana-rahasya 2.7ff.

anartha nivritti (*anartha-nivṛtti*)—“the dissipation of obstacles.” This is the fourth step of devotional advancement. See *Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu* 1.4.10-11.

*antaraṅga—*intimate, close*.*

*antaraṅga-śakti—*internal energy = Chit Shakti

*anubhāva—*One of the components of the rasa-experience. The anubhavas consist of all the conscious physical responses to inner emotion.

*anurāga—*“enthusiasm, spontaneous attraction.” HNC 12.

*antarmukha—*turned inward, introspective. The opposite of *bahirmukha.*

*antyaja—*See outcaste.

*anusmṛti—*constant recollection; also elsewhere called *dhruvānusmṛti.* Jiva refers to Ramanuja’s commentary on Vs. 1.1.1. HNC 15.93.

*āpana-daśā—*The stage of appropriation. The stage in *bhāva-sādhana* where one makes the spiritual identity one’s own. Also called *bhāvāpana-daśā* and *svarūpa-siddhi.* From *Jaiva Dharma* (602): “First chant the Holy Name with enthusiasm. Then add a feeling of ownership to this enthusiasm. Then add a feeling of intimacy to this sense of ownership. Then, as your attitude becomes purified, you will come to the stage where you fully appropriate the eleven aspects of your spiritual identity, or *bhāvāpana-daśā*. Throughout the stage of remembering, one has only superimposed the spiritual identity on himself. At the stage of appropriation, however, pure identification with the spiritual body is attained.”

aparadh (*aparādha*)*—“*offense.” This is one of the anarthas. It too takes four forms: offense to Krishna’s name, His forms, His devotees and to the living beings in general. There are ten offenses to the Holy Name and numerous offenses to the Lord’s deity form. The principal and most dangerous offense is *vaiṣṇavāparādha*, or that committed against Krishna’s devotees. The aparadhs are mostly based on an incorrect understanding of sambandha jnana. BVT takes the opportunity to explain each and every aspect from Varnashram on.

*aprakaṭa-līlā—*Krishna’s pastimes that are invisible to this world.

arati (*ārati*)—This is a Bengali term derived from the Sanskrit *ā-rātrika*, which technically means “light ceremony performed to dissipate the night.” This ceremony, in which a ghee lamp and other items are offered to the Deity by waving them before him, is performed at several junctures during the day and night. The arati ceremony is an example of mantra-mayi smaran (q.v.).

archan (*arcana*)—Deity worship. One of the five most powerful devotional practices. The principal devotional activity recommended in the Pancharatrika school of Vaishnavism.

*artha—“*worth, value, meaning, wealth, purpose.” One of the four *puruṣārthas* (q.v.).

*artha-pravṛtti—*A term used by Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Thakur to mean a positive engagement in meditation on Krishna, in distinction to *anartha-nivṛtti,* which is the work one does on eliminating the obstacles and negative elements of one’s character in devotional practice.

*artha-vāda—*to think that the description of the Holy Name’s glories in the scriptures is not entirely true, but a kind of hyperbole only meant to incite people to chant. See HNC 8.

*āsakti*—“attachment.” This is fifth stage of spiritual advancement, when one becomes attached to devotional practices and unable to live without them.

*āśā-bandha*—The characteristic of the devotee to always hope for Krishna’s mercy despite feelings of personal worthlessness.

*asat-saṅga—*Association with the impious.

*asat-tṛṣṇā—“*hankering for the impermanent.” One of the anarthas described in HNC 3.13 and *Bhajana-rahasya* 2.9: “Inauspicious desires for this-worldly or next-worldly pleasures, mystic powers and liberation are the four kinds of *asat-tṛṣṇā.*”

ashram (*āśrama*)—(1) hermitage, or place where devotees cultivating spiritual life reside; “a sacred sanctuary.” (2) One of the four stages of life according to the Varnashram system (q.v.)

*āśraya­—*Literally, “shelter, refuge.” Has the technical meaning of the “reservoir of love.”

*āśraya-tattva*— An eternal associate of the Lord who is the divine shelter principle for the conditioned souls. The jiva must take shelter of a raganuga devotee in his specific rasa to enter the eternal pastimes of the Lord.

*āśraya-vigraha*—Radharani, the ultimate source of all devotion and therefore the original “reservoir of love” for Krishna.

ashta-kaliya lila (*aṣṭa-kālīya-līlā*)*—“*pastimes in the eight parts of the day; the Lord’s daily cycle of pastimes; or circadian pastimes.” According to Bhaktivinoda Thakur, this is one of the aspects of *bhāva-tattva.* Descriptions of this lila can be found in the *Sanat-kumāra-saṁhitā,* which is quoted in full in *Jaiva Dharma* and *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta*, and in the *Smaraṇa-maṅgala-stotra,* which is given as the last verse of each chapter of *Bhajana-rahasya*, and in *Saṅkalpa-kalpa-druma*.

*aṣṭa-sakhī*—Radharani’s eight girlfriends, also known as *parama-preṣṭha sakhīs.* Their names are Lalita, Vishakha, Champakalata, Chitra, Tungavidya, Indulekha, Rangadevi and Sudevi.

atma (*ātman*)—(1) the self or soul (=jiva); (2) the Supreme Self.

*ātma-nivedana—*Self surrender. This is the sixth and final step of taking shelter of Krishna (see chapter 13). It is also the last of the nine processes of devotional service described in the Bhagavatam (reference)

*ātma-rati—* “pleasure in the self, pleasure in spiritual life.” HNC 8.25-26.

*audāsīnya—“*indifference, apathy” The first of the three kinds of carelessness in chanting. See HNC 12.

*avidyā*—Ignorance. The external energy.

## —b—

*bahiraṅga—“*excluded, external, not intimate or close”; opposite of *antaraṅga.*

*bahiraṅga-śakti—*external energy = Maya Shakti

*bahirmukha*—Literally, “face outside.” Used for one who is “facing away from” or has “turned his back” on Krishna. Other translations: disinterested, estranged, excluded, worldly minded, extroverted, etc. Synonym, *vimukha.* The opposite is *antarmukha* (“introverted”) or *–unmukha* (“turned towards”), as in *kṛṣṇonmukha, bhajanonmukha*, *sevonmukha,* etc.

bhajan (*bhajana*)—This is often translated as “worship,” but this translation is fairly inadequate. It in fact indicates direct devotional service, but particularly as manifest through meditative chanting of the Holy Names.

*bhajana-naipuṇya—* “Expertise in bhajan means quickly removing anarthas by diligent practice and through the blessings of the spiritual master.” (*sādhana-yogenācārya-prasādena ca tūrṇaṁ tad-apanayanam eva bhajana-naipuṇyam*, *Āmnāya-sūtra* 75).

bhakti—Devotion; the affective path to realizing the Supreme Truth; the constitutional function of the living being to serve Krishna. Bhakti is of three types: *sādhanā-bhakti, bhāva-bhakti* and *prema-bhakti* (q.v.).

*bhāva*—(1) state, condition; (2) emotion, feeling, mood; (3) the first level of spiritual perfection, at which stage one’s relation with Krishna is firmly established (*bhāva-bhakti*); (4) the different attitudes in relation with Krishna (*sthāyi-bhāvas,* q.v.) as well as some of the other ingredients in the makeup of rasa (*vyabhicāri-bhāvas, sāttvika-bhāvas,* q.v.) (HNC 15.28); (5) the eleven aspects of the siddha deha (*ekādaśa-bhāva*, q.v.). (6) “thought” = *bhāvanā.*

The word *bhāva* derives from the verb “to be or become”; its basic meaning is thus “state.” Since this can be a state of being or a state of emotions, the word can also be translated as “feeling” or “emotion.” This is the usual understanding when we talk about reaching the spiritual level called *bhāva* (*Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.3.1), at which stage one is said to first experience involuntary ecstatic symptoms (*sāttvikas*). Thus some translate *bhāva* as ecstasy. However, when Bhaktivinoda Thakur speaks about the *bhāva-mārga* (q.v.) or *bhāvāpana-daśā* or *bhāva-sādhanā,* or *nija-bhāva* (“one’s own *bhāva*”), he is refering to the eternal spiritual identity. There is a clear connection between these two:

The stage of *bhāva* is clearly identified with *rati* by Rupa Goswami. Thus, attaining one’s spiritual identity in *svarūpa-siddhi* is (i.e. *āpana-daśā* is simultaneously *bhāva.*). Thus *bhāva-sādhanā* means the specific cultivation of one’s spiritual identity.

*bhāva-mārga—*“the path of cultivating one’s spiritual identity” (Others have translated as “the path of ecstasy, or ecstatic love”). In *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta* (p.314), Bhaktivinoda also uses the term *bhāvanā-mārga*, “the path of contemplation” where he defines *bhāvanā* as *siddha-deha-bhāvanā:* “While still in this body, the practitioner serves in Krishna’s daily pastimes in his mind and, upon arriving at the point of *svarūpa-siddhi*, identifies completely with this spiritual identity.” He then goes on to explain how to think of that body. Synonym: *bhāva-sādhanā,* “cultivating the spiritual identity.”

bhāvāpana-daśā—See āpana-daśā.

bhāva-sevā—“mental service.” HNC chapter 14.

bhāva-tattva—Bhaktivinoda Thakur divides into two categories: the sādhaka’s *ekādaśa-bhāva* and *kṛṣṇa-līlā*. The first of these is related to the process, the latter to the goal.

*bhāvuka—*“an ecstatic devotee”

*bhoga—*(1) enjoyment, especially sense enjoyment. (2) experiencing of any sort, good or bad. (3) The food offerings one makes to the Deity, before it becomes prasad.

*bhūmā—*“great.” See HNC 11, note 10.

bija (*bīja*)—seed; the seeds of karma, i.e. desire.

*bīja-mantra*—See mantra.

*brahmacārī* (brahmachari)—celibate student; the first of the four stations of life in the Varnashram system.

*brahmajyoti*—The effulgence emanating from the Lord’s body

Brahman (*brahman*)—(1) The first stage of God-realization as an impersonal, formless, all-pervading, substratum of pure consciousness. (2) Vedic hymn.

Brahmana (*brāhmaṇa*)—A class of Shruti scriptures that explains the proper chanting of the Vedic mantras.

Brahmin (*brāhmaṇa*)—the highest of the four castes of the Varnashram system; the priestly and intellectual class.

## —c—

*caturvyūha*—The four principle Vishnu expansions in the Vaikuntha spiritual world: Vasudeva, Sankarshan, Pradyumna, Aniruddha.

Chaitanya (1486-1534)—The incarnation of Krishna who established the chanting of the Holy Names as the religious practice of the Age of Kali; the combined form of Radha and Krishna; Krishna in the mood and bodily hue of Srimati Radharani. Other names: Krishna Chaitanya, Mahaprabhu, Gauranga, Gaura Hari, Sachinandan, Sachisuta, etc. His main biographies are *Caitanya-bhāgavata* and *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. The latter is a very important theological treatise for the followers of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu.

*chāyā-nāmābhāsa*—“the shadow of the Holy Name.” One of the two kinds of Namabhasa (q.v.). There are two kinds of Namabhasa, *chāyā* and *pratibimba* (q.v.). The “shadow” of the Holy Name is the result of ignorance, while the “reflection” of the Name is the result of deliberate error. The former has a positive effect, the latter not. See HNC 3.56ff.

chit shakti (*cic-chakti*)—Krishna’s spiritual energy.

*cintāmaṇi*—“the philosopher’s stone, wish-fulfilling gem.”

## —d—

*dākṣiṇya—*submissiveness and the desire to please. These are the characteristics of the *dakṣiṇā nāyikā*. Opposite of *vāmya.*

darshan (*darśana*)—(1) seeing, especially seeing the Deity form of the Lord; (2) meeting, as in seeing and speaking to a saintly person; (3) A philosophical system, or way of “seeing” the world.

*daśa-mūla—*The *“*ten basic teachings” of Lord Chaitanya. See HNC 7.12, note 4.

*dāsya—*The mood of service. This is both one of the fixed relationships with Krishna (*sthāyi-bhāvas*) and one of the nine ways of cultivating consciousness of Krishna (*nava-vidhā bhakti*).

Deity, deity form—Term used by devotees to designate the Lord’s *arcā* form, or murti (*mūrti*). This term is prefered over “idol,” which is sometimes used, but is considered to have negative connotations. The Lord’s deity form is said to be of many types: in stone, wood, metal, sand, paint, or mental.

Devi dham (*devī-dhāma*)—“the abode of the Goddess.” The goddess refered to here is Maya Devi, or Durga, who is the titulary deity of the material world.

dham *(dhāma*)—(1) effulgence; (2) abode, home. This term is especially used to refer to the places where Krishna has His pastimes—Vrindavan and Nabadwip, and to a lesser extent, Dvaraka and Puri.

*dhāraṇā—*(1) The sixth of the eight parts of the Patanjali yoga system. (2) The second of the stages of smaran on the *bhāva-mārga*. “self-reminding.” On this stage one works at attaining steadiness in remembering. HNC 15.93.

*dharma—*(1) the inherent qualities of a substance; (2) righteousness; (3) religious duty, (4) religion.

*dharma*-*dhvajī—*Literally, “One who flies the flag of religion.” A person who puts on a show of piety, a hypocrite.

*dhruvānusmṛti—*See *anusmṛti.*

dhyan (*dhyāna*)—“meditation.” The sixth of the eight parts of the Patanjali yoga system and third of the five stages of smaran on the *bhāva-mārga*. See HNC 15.93.

diksha (*dīkṣā*)—initiation.

diksha guru (*dīkṣā guru*)*—*The initiating spiritual master.

## —e—

*ekādaśa-bhāva—*The eleven aspects of the devotee’s spiritual form, in which she performs mental service to the Divine Couple. These are described in chapter 15, and are originally found in a verse in Gopal Guru Goswami’s *Paddhati.* For supplementary descriptions, one can look to *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta,* p. 315ff.

Ekadasi (*ekādaśī*)—The eleventh day of the phases of the moon. This a day of fasting for Vaishnavas, also known as Hari-vasara.

## \<em\>—\</em\>g—

Garbhodakashayi Vishnu—The second expansion of the Purushavatars, governing the creation of the individual universes. Corresponds to Pradyumna of the Chaturvyuha.

*gauṇa-nāma­*—God’s secondary names, specifically those that are based on His relation to material creation as its creator, maintainer and destroyer, or as the savior of the conditioned souls. (See HNC 2.42ff).

*gauṇa-patha—*Secondary or indirect paths for attaining devotion to Krishna. These are paths of jnana, karma or yoga mixed with devotion. Neither jnana nor karma on their own can lead one to Krishna, but by combining them with bhakti one can make some progress. (HNC 1.62ff)

*gauṇa-upāya—*Secondary means. The same as *gauṇa-patha.*

grihastha (*gṛhastha*)—“householder.” The second of the four ashrams in the Varnashram system.

guna (*guṇa*)—“quality”; (1) the three qualities or modes of material nature: passion (*rajas*), goodness (*sattva*) and ignorance (*tamas*). (2) the virtues or qualities of the Lord, which are progressively revealed to the sadhaka through the Holy Name. (3) In *Jaiva Dharma* (p. 592), Bhaktivinoda Thakur gives this as an alternative to the sixth *bhāva* of the *ekādaśa bhāva*: “In order to perform your specific service, you will need expertise in various skills and talents, as well as appropriate personal qualities and garments. The spiritual master will indicate to you what these are.”

Gunavatar (*guṇāvatāra*)—The three gods who govern the modes of material nature: Brahma, who oversees creation, or the mode of passion, Vishnu, the maintainer (mode of goodness), and Shiva the destroyer (mode of ignorance).

guru—teacher, spiritual master. See diksha guru, siksha guru, Sat Guru

## —h—

*hārām—*This Arabic word means “taboo” or “unlawful.” Muslims in Bengal sometimes use the word as an expletive. According to Hari Das Thakur, because this sound resembles a way of calling out to Rama (*hā rāma*) it is an example of *saṅketa-nāmābhāsa* (q.v.). See CC 3.3.53.

Hari—name of God. See the appendix on the explanations of the Maha Mantra.

*hari-vāsara*—See Ekadasi.

*helā* or *helanam—*One of the four kinds of Namabhasa named in SB 6.2.14. Careless or disparaging chanting of the Holy Name (HNC 3.45, note 12). In 3.48, Bhaktivinoda Thakur says that this is the worst of the four kinds of Namabhasa, indicating that it means more than mere carelessness, but includes a degree of contempt or disregard for the Holy Name.

*hṛdaya*-*daurbalya*—“weakness of heart.” One of the four kinds of anartha mentioned in HNC 3.13 and *Bhajana-rahasya* 2.11. Includes trivial attachments, fault-finding, envy, and the desire for personal aggrandizement.

*hlādinī—*The pleasure-giving potency. One of the three aspects of the spiritual energy, which brings joy to the Lord and His devotees. This energy is personified in Radha, who is known as *hlādinī-śakti-svarūpiṇī.*

## \<em\>—i—\</em\>

*īśvara*—This is the term that most accurately translates as “God.” There are many other words in Sanskritic languages like Bengali that also mean God; some used in this book are *vibhu, sarveśvara*, etc. Krishna, etc., are names of God. Bhaktivinoda Thakur distinguishes between primary (*mukhya*) and secondary (*gauṇa*) names of God (q.v.).

## \<em\>—\</em\>j—

*jāḍya—*“lethargy, sloth.” One of the three categories of inattentiveness. See HNC 12.

*jāta rati = bhāva daśā*. I have translated as stage of ecstasies. Prema as stage of ecstatic love. Stage of spiritual emotion.

japa—Chanting a mantra while counting each time one repeats the mantra. Japa is of three types: *vācika, upāṁśu* and *mānasa.* (q.v.)

japa mala (*japa-mālā*)—A rosary of 108 beads, preferably made of tulasi wood, used for chanting the Maha Mantra. According to the instructions found in *Hari-bhakti-vilāsa,* the mala should taper off, with larger beads at one end, progressively getting smaller. One principal bead symbolizing the Divine Couple marks the beginning point. The other beads symbolize the gopis who participated in the Rasa dance.

jiva (*jīva*)—The individual living being or soul. Krishna’s marginal potency.

*jīve dayā*—Compassion for all living beings. Bhaktivinoda wrote about his initiation. “When my spiritual master came and performed the initiation rituals, I became cheerful. From that day on the sin of meat eating vanished from my heart and I began to feel a little compassion toward all beings.”

jnana (*jñāna*)—1. consciousness, knowledge, awareness, gnosis, wisdom; 2. the path of cultivating spiritual knowledge.

jnana kanda (*jñāna-kāṇḍa*)—The section of the scriptures that promotes philosophical speculation about the Supreme Truth.

jnani (*jñānī*)—(1) a knowledgeable person; (2) a philosopher following the path of jnana. (3) a monist, philosopher who believes the impersonal aspect of Godhead (Brahman) is the highest.

## —k—

*kaivalya—“*aloneness, perfect isolation; absolute unity.” The name given by the Jains and yogis to the state of liberation (see *Yoga-sūtra* 2.25, 3.50, 3.55). It refers to the state of the individual soul when separated from all material conditioning and situated in perfect isolation (*svarūpa-pratiṣṭhā*—*Yoga-sūtra* 4.34).

kama (*kāma*)—(1) “desire, lust”; (2) Eros, Cupid, the god of love; (3) sensual pleasure, i.e. the third of the four goals of human life (See *puruṣārtha.*). This term is usually limited to material sensual or sexual desire, but is also used to refer to the transcendental erotic love manifested in Krishna’s Vrindavan pastimes, where love only takes the appearance of lusty desire. (See *Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.2.285.)

kanishtha adhikari (*kaniṣṭha adhikārī*)*—*A person on the lowest level of eligibility for devotional service; “one whose faith is weak” *(Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.2.19).

kanishtha Vaishnava (*kaniṣṭha vaiṣṇava*)*—*A neophyte on the devotional path. One who has a purely sectarian mentality, whose accepts the need to serve Krishna, but is unable to recognize His devotees or discriminate between them and non-devotees. See SB 11.2.47 and HNC 4, note 19.

*karma­—*(1) action, work; (2) a path of spiritual life that is based in pious work and ritual performances. *Karma-yoga* (q.v.) refers to the renunciation of the fruits of such actions. (3) fruitive activity; (4) the consequences of action, the “law of retribution.” There are four types of karma: *bīja, kūṭa, phalonmukha* and *prārabdha* (q.v.). See *Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.1.14.

karma kanda (*karma-kāṇḍa*)—The section of the Vedic literature that supports the process of rituals, especially sacrifices, as a means of spiritual advancement. The section of the scriptures that promotes religious ritual.

*karma-jaḍa*—A term used to refer to blind engaging in fruitive activity.

kirtan (*kīrtana*)—The loud glorification of the Lord’s names, virtues and pastimes. The terms is sometimes loosely used to indicate chanting the Holy Names on beads also. This is the second of the nine activities of devotional service.

*krama—*step, in the sense of a step-by-step program. “procedure, gradual path” *krame krame* means “gradually.”

Krishna—The principal name of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. See the appendix giving explanations of the Maha Mantra.

*kṛṣṇa-saṁsāra—*a Krishna-centered householder life; life in the world lived on behalf of Krishna (see HNC 2.64-68).

Kshatriya (*kṣatriya*)—the martial and political caste.

Kshirodakasayi Vishnu—The third Purushavatar form of the Lord, corresponding to Aniruddha of the Chaturvyuha. This form of Godhead is the all-pervading Supersoul.

*kula guru— A* spiritual master coming in a family that has traditionally given initiation to members of a client family. See HNC 6.41.

*kūṭa*—the dormant state of karma, where the acts that one has performed await the fructification of their results.

## —l—

lakh *(lakṣa*)—100,000.

*lakṣaṇā vṛtti—*inferred or secondary meaning; figurative or metaphorical interpretation. (HNC 7 note 9)

*laukikī śraddhā—“*conventional faith.” i.e. faith that is grounded in social convention rather than knowledge of the scriptures, etc. See HNC 3.57.

lila (*līlā*)—the transcendental pastimes, play or activities of the Lord.

lila smaran (*līlā-smaraṇa*)—the practice of remembering Krishna’s pastimes, especially in their *aṣṭa-kālīya* form.

*liṅga-deha—“*The subtle body.” *liṅga* literally means “characteristic sign.” The subtle body contains the subtle aspects of the individual being—his desires and conditioning as well as his accumulated karma. It undergoes changes and evolves, and does not dissipate with the death of the gross body, only coming to an end when the soul’s material condition comes to an end.

*lobha—“*greed, intense longing, yearning.” This, rather than intellectual conviction or sense of obligation, is the qualification for engaging in raganuga bhakti. *Jaiva Dharma* (599): “When one reaches this particular stage of spiritual advancement, it is absolutely necessary to listen to Hari katha in the proper sequence, hearing the *nitya lila* separately from the *naimittika*. By so doing, the beauty of Krishna’s pastimes becomes evident and the tendency to engage in raganuga bhakti arises in the heart of the hearer. The hearer then thinks, ‘Oh! How wonderful is Subala’s mood of friendship! I would like to serve Krishna in the same mood as he!’ Such a tendency is called *lobha*.”

## \<em\>—m—\</em\>

madhura rasa—The conjugal or erotic mood of divine love.

*mādhurya—*(1) Sweetness. (2) Intimacy, in contrast with *aiśvarya* (q.v.), where God is perceived in His anthropomorphic aspect rather than in His divine splendor. (3) the conjugal mood or erotic sentiment. (See madhura rasa).

Madhvacharya—1200-1300. The founder of the dualistic school of Vaishnava theology. Chaitanya Mahaprabhu and His followers are affiliated with the Madhva school through initiation.

*madhyama adhikārī—*One with the intermediate qualifications for devotional practice, i.e. “one who has limited knowledge of the scriptures, but nevertheless possesses faith.” (*Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.2.18).

*madhyama-vaiṣṇava—“*the intermediate Vaishnava” described in SB 11.2.44: characterized by four behaviors: love for God, friendship with devotees, compassion for the suffering innocent, and indifference to the atheistic. See HNC chapter 4. Note 20.

Maha Vishnu—The Purushavatar that is the source of all universal creation, also known as Karanarnavashayi Vishnu and Maha Sankarshan.

Maha Mantra— “the great mantra”: Though this term is sometimes used to describe other mantras, for Gaudiyas it refers principally to the mantra of sixteen names and thirty-two syllables: Hare Krishna Hare Krishna Krishna Krishna Hare Hare Hare Rama Hare Rama Rama Rama Hare Hare. See mantra.

*mahā-pātaka—*mortal sins.

manjari (*mañjarī*)—lit. “flowerbud.” The name given to a class of Radharani’s friends or sakhis, whose affection for Radha exceeds their devotion to even Krishna, who pride themselves in serving Radharani and organizing and elaborating the loving pastimes of the Divine Couple. They are somewhat younger than Radharani’s other friends, usually between 10 and 12 years old. Rupa Manjari is chief amongst them.

mantra*—*(1) spell, incantation; (2) sacred syllables that liberate the mind. Generally speaking, mantras are an integral part of the Tantric or Pancharatra systems of worship and initiation in the mantra is a necessary prerequisite to archan (q.v.). It includes a *b*ī*ja-mantra*, a single-syllable that in itself usually has no meaning but is specifically related to a particular deity (e.g., *klīṁ* for Krishna), the name of the Deity in the dative case (e.g., *kṛṣṇāya*) and a word of salutation or self-surrender (e.g., *namaḥ*). A number of rules govern the mantra: one must chant in a state of ritual purity at specific times of the day, one must count the number of times one chants, etc. In this sense, the Maha Mantra is technically not a mantra, but a verse consisting of the Lord’s names in the vocative case. Nevertheless, the followers of Lord Chaitanya normally take a vow to chant the Maha Mantra a certain number of times every day.

*mantra-dhyāna-mayī—*Krishna’s pastimes when manifested as a “still picture,” where Krishna and His intimate associates assemble. See HNC 15.45.

*markaṭa-vairāgya—“*monkey renunciation”; the false renunciation of someone who adopts the dress of a monk while still harboring desires to enjoy sense gratification. The monkey wears no clothes, has no home and eats only fruits and roots, yet it seizes any chance to engage in sexual activity or other sense gratificatory activity. This is the source of the term. See HNC 4.28 and 9.35. From CC 2.16.278.

Maya (*māyā*)—illusion. Also personified as Maya Devi.

Maya shakti (*māyā-śakti*)—the Lord’s external energy. Cf. *bahiraṅga-śakti.*

Mayavada (*māyāvāda*)*—*“the philosophy of illusion” associated with Advaitavada. Since the Absolute is unitary, any appearance of duality or multiplicity is illusory. One who follows this doctrine is called a Mayavadi.

Mleccha—This word is used for non-Hindu foreigners, primarily Muslims. It indicates impure habits, especially meat-eating. Even Mlecchas can be liberated by chanting the semblance of the Holy Name, and of course, Mleccha converts like Hari Das himself can reach the highest levels of Vaishnava sainthood.

*mukhya*-*nāma—*The primary or principal names of the Lord, which pertain to His pastimes or lila (q.v.). HNC 2.

*mukhya-upāya—*Opposite of *gauṇa-upāya.* “Principal means.”

## —n—

*nāma—*Lit. “name.” (1) The Holy Name; (2) One’s name in the siddha deha, i.e. the third of the eleven aspects of that identity. In *Jaiva Dharma,* Gopal Guru Goswami states: “After hearing about the activities of the gopis, you develop an attraction for a particular service. The name of the maidservant who is perfectly suited to that service is your name. The name your spiritual master gives you after examining your tendencies is your name eternally. The beautiful girls of Vraja will delight in addressing you by that name.”

*nāmābhāsa—*Namabhasa. “the semblance”facsimile of the Holy Name” An *ābhāsa* is like a blurred image. Other translations include “shadow” and “reflection” of the Holy Name. These two words, however, have closer corresponding words in Bengali, *chāyā* and *pratibimba*, both of which are used to describe particular kinds of *nāmābhāsa.* Therefore “semblance” is the best translation. Sarvabhavana has also translated as “the unclarified name.” It is of four kinds, *saṅketa, parihāsa, stobha* and *helā* (q.v.), which progressively weaken the effects of the Holy Name. See HNC 3.38 and *Jaiva Dharma*, chapter 25.

Namacharya (*nāmācārya*)—The exemplary teacher of the Holy Name. The title given to Hari Das Thakur.

*nāma-guru—*The person who teaches us about the Holy Name, tells us of its supremacy, and then initiates one in the Holy Name or the mantra, which also is made up of names of the Lord. The initiating spiritual master or diksha guru is also the Nama-guru.

*nāmāparādha—“*offenses to the Holy Name.”

Narayan—The name of God in His majestic aspect, who resides in Vaikuntha.

Nimbarka—of uncertain date. The founder of one of the four Vaishnava sampradayas.

nishtha (*niṣṭhā*)*—*This term is often translated as strong faith. It means full commitment, dedication to a practice. “fixed determination” “steadfastness”

*nitya-siddha*—eternally perfect; those associates of the Lord who have never come into contact with matter,i.e., have never known anything but love for Krishna.

## —o—

om—The sacred syllable of the Vedic literature that is said to be the Lord’s holy name. Also known as *praṇava*.

outcaste (*antyaja)* = The Indian Varnashram system in practice is based on birth. Those who are born into Hinduism have a caste status that is determined by birth. Those who are not born into the four castes, Brahmin, Kshatriya, Vaishya or Shudra, are considered “outcastes”, or *antyaja.* These people were traditionally assimilated into the Hindu castes as Shudras through adopting service trades. Bhaktivinoda Thakur was writing for the Indian situation, which does not seem to apply for converts from other societies. In fact, in earlier days of Hindu expansion, non-Hindu kings, etc., were assimilated into Hinduism as Kshatriyas, etc. In the same way, a modern understanding of caste and Varnashram requires that individuals, in whatever country they find themselves, work according to their nature and earn an honest and ethical living thereby. Those who teach or act as priests or spiritual guides may be considered as Brahmins.

## —p—

*pāda-sevana—*“serving the Lord’s feet.” One of the nine types of devotional service (*nava-vidh}a bhakti*).

*pālya-dāsī—*The last of the eleven aspects of the siddha deha. According to Dhyana Chandra, this refers to the sense of complete surrender to Lalita or another of the intimate friends of Radharani. In *Jaiva Dharma* (p. 594), Bhaktivinoda Thakur cites *Vraja-vilāsa-stava* (29) as an example of this bhava: “May Lalita Devi, who is flooded with the juices of intense love, who has developed a mood of boldness and confidence out of deep affection, who daily assists her beloved Radha and Krishna, who are dearer to her than life itself, in meeting at their place of rendez-vous, and who with great expertise always teaches her girlfriend Radha the art of loving pique, accept me within her own group.”

Pancharatra (*pañcarātra*)—“five nights.” One of the two principal approaches to worshiping Vishnu or Krishna, based on ritual and Deity worship.

*pāpa*—“sin.” Acts leading to negative or inauspicious karma. It has three aspects. See HNC 9.5. There are five degrees of sinful act, culminating in the *mahā-pātaka.*

*parākāṣṭhā—*“highest aspiration.” The tenth of the eleven aspects of the siddha deha. It refers to the dream of the manjari to receive a particular special grace from the Divine Couple. In *Jaiva Dharma,* Bhaktivinoda refers to *Vilāpa-kusumāñjali* 100 and 102 as examples of *parākāṣṭhāśvāsa*, e.g.: “O Lord! O nectar moon of Gokula! Your cheerful face is like a blooming lotus flower, O sweetly smiling one, melting with compassion! Please lead me to the place where You and Your beloved enjoy Your delightful loving pastimes.” (100)

*pārakīya-rasa—*the illicit relationship of a woman with another’s husband. Bhaktivinoda writes that any identity which is contrary to one’s pure spiritual inclination to serve Krishna is a type of “illicit relationship” that must be overcome. Mahaprabhu wrote to Rupa Goswami about this mood: “A woman who is attached to another man continues to engage in her household duties, but internally she is relishing the elixir of her new love relation.” (CC Madhya 1.211)

*paramparā—*Literally, “from one to the next.” Disciplic succession.

Para Shakti (*parā śakti*)—Krishna’s higher, internal potency, manifested in three aspects, *sandhinī, saṁvit* and *hlādinī*. (q.v.)

*pariṇāma-vāda—*The Vaishnava philosophy of “transformation of energies.” The Mayavadis hold that the observable universe is in no way different from the Absolute, but only a illusory projection onto Reality. This is called *vivarta-vāda.* The Vaishnavas, however, hold that God’s energies are real though temporary.

*parihāsa—*jokingly, jest or ridicule. One of the four kinds of *nāmābhāsa* (q.v.). See HNC 3.38.

*prabhāva—*influence. See HNC 1.

*prakaṭa-līlā—*the manifest lila, Krishna’s pastimes in this world. Bhaktivinoda Thakur uses the term to apply to the sadhaka devotee’s condition. While in the material world, the relationship one develops with Krishna. (See note 31 chapter 15)

*prākṛta—*worldly or mundane. Use to describe the devotee of the lowest order. (See *kaniṣṭha-vaiṣṇava*)

*pramāṇa—*“authoritative proof or evidence.” Though Indian epistemology accepts numerous valid proofs in rational argument, Vaishnavas accept scripture alone above all.

*prameya—*axioms demonstrated in the Vedic literature, of which there are nine according to the *daśa-mūla* (q.v.)*.*

*prārabdha—*Lit. “already begun.” These are the reactions to past activities that one has already begun to experience in the form of a particular kind of birth and everything that goes with it. Also *prārabdha karma,* “old karma.”

*pratibimba-nāmābhāsa—Pratibimba* means reflection. Bhaktivinoda Thakur uses this term to signify a chanting of the Holy Name that is rooted in a false or distorted faith, i.e. an understanding of God as an impersonal truth. See HNC 3.56ff.

*prāyaścitta*—atonement or penance. Scriptures prescribe various ways of getting rid of the burden of sin, such as fasting, giving in charity, etc. These are another burden for devotees if there is no connection to devotional activity. Devotion itself is all that is needed to destroy sin. (See HNC 9)

*prayojana—“*necessity”. Gaudiya Vaishnavas understand this as the ultimate goal or result of spiritual practice. It is the driving need of the spiritual being to attain unity with God in all its variegated manifestations. It is thus identified with prema (q.v.).

prema (*prema*)—Lit. “love.” The eighth and final stage of devotion. It is the first of the higher stages of divine love. Because it is accompanied by ecstatic symptoms it is often called “ecstatic love.” Because it is distinct from human love, it is also called “divine love.”

*premārurukṣu*— “One desiring to attain prema.” Used in *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta*.

purascharan (*puraścaraṇa*)—A dedicated vow to chant the diksha mantra a certain number of times. This is generally said to be necessary to “breathe life” into the mantra. See *Hari-bhakti-vilāsa*, chapter 17.

purushartha (*puruṣārtha*)*—“*goals of human life”: finding one’s life’s duty or vocation (*dharma*); finding financial and social success (*artha*); finding love and sensual satisfaction (*kāma*); and finding spiritual salvation (*mokṣa*). Love of God is called the fifth goal of life (*pañcama-puruṣārtha*). Also *pumartha.*

Purushavatar *(puruṣāvatāra*)*—*The three incarnations of Vishnu that deal with the universal creation—Maha Vishnu, Garbhodakashayi Vishnu and Kshirodakashayi Vishnu. (HNC 5)

## —r—

raganuga (*rāgānugā*) bhakti—Devotion that is impelled by natural desire rather than by logical argument or external pressure. Such devotion is usually characterized by the desire to follow specific eternal associates of the Lord.

*rāgānuga-krama*—In the *smaraṇa-daśā* (q.v.), meditation on the circadian pastimes of the Divine Couple impelled by desire rather than out of a lesser motivation. Not to be confused with raganuga bhakti itself, as smaran of the type being discussed there is an anga of raganuga bhakti.

Ramananda Raya—One of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu’s closest companions in Jagannath Puri. Mahaprabhu met Ramananda in Vidyanagar in present day Andhra Pradesh, which at the time was under the rule of the Gajapati kings of Orissa. Ramananda explained the Gita Govinda and othr aspects of devotion to the Divine Couple, Radha and Krishna, to Mahaprabhu.

Ramanuja (1017-1137)—founder of the Sri Sampradaya and author of the *viśiṣṭādvaita* interpretation of Vedanta.

*rasa—*Lit. “juice, sap, nectar” or “taste, flavor,” this word has a long history in Sanskrit poetics. Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Thakur innovated “mellows” as a translation and this usage has become current through the widespread publications of Bhaktivedanta Swami. It has usefulness as a totally new usage of the word “mellow” and as such there can be no objection to its use. Scholars often translate the word as “sentiment” or “aesthetic experience.” Some other devotees use “sacred rapture.” All of uses are possible according to the context, but I have prefered to use the word rasa where the specific devotional experience specific to a particular relationship with Krishna is concerned. The word is not to be confused with *rāsa*, as in Krishna’s *rāsa* dance. As a technical term, rasa means the aesthetic or spiritual experience that develops out of a combination of various ingredients, the basis of which is a feeling of love for Krishna. See HNC 15.7 with footnote.

rasika (*rasika*)—One who seeks and relishes rasa. The term is used of both the mundane and the transcendental rasas.

*rati—*Lit. “love”; especially erotic love. The term has specific meaning in aesthetics, however, meaning the same as *sthāyi-bhāva* (q.v.).

rishi (*ṛṣi*)—“seers”. The seers to whom the Vedic knowledge was revealed; the authors of the Vedic literature and the Vedic mantras.

ruchi (*ruci)—“*taste or inclination,” as in “to have a taste for something” (not like rasa, which means the flavor itself.) (1) Bhaktivinoda defines this as “the tendency that arises out of the merit one has accumulated in the present and previous lives.” In modern terms this would mean the tendencies coming out of one’s religious actions, both through nature and nurture. (2) This is also the name of a state of spiritual advancement, following dedication and preceding attachment, when one starts to experience spontaneous enthusiasm for devotional activities. According to Jiva Goswami (*Bhakti-sandarbha* 312-314), raganuga bhaktas who have not attained the stage of ruchi are advised to practice a mixture of raganuga (q.v.) and vaidhi bhakti (q.v.), i.e., devotion that is motivated by the positive desire to attain direct service in a particular rasa, or devotion that is motivated by scriptural injunctions and logical arguments.

## —s—

sadhaka (*sādhaka*)—A spiritual practitioner seeking perfection, or siddhi.

sadhana (*sādhana*)—Spiritual practice.

sadhana siddha (*sādhana*-*siddha*)*—*One who has become perfected through practice.

sadhu (*sādhu*)—“a good person.” A saintly person. Generally used by Vaishnavas to mean a Vaishnava. (HNC 4.44ff)

sadhu sanga (*sādhu-saṅga*)—“the company of the saintly.” Association with devotees. One of the steps towards attaining prema.

sahaja (*sahaja*)*—*“easy or natural.” See *Bhajana-rahasya,* Chapter 6.

Sahajiya (*sahajīyā*)—(1) A heterodox sect that considers Tantric sexual practices to be the real purport of the Vaishnava scriptures describing the activities of the Divine Couple. Sometimes called Prakrita Sahajiyas. (2) A term used more widely to include all those who have a mundane concept of Krishna’s divine romantic pastimes, or who think that they can attain them without the requisite moral purification.

*sahṛdaya—*“one who has heart”; the appreciative audience of Sanskrit poetry or dramatic works. The *sahṛdaya* is a person who has the education and culture to appreciate the subtleties of such works and is also situated in the mode of goodness and thus able to concentrate on what is being said, thus allowing the full force of rasa to manifest in him. (See HNC 15.32 fn.)

samsara (*saṁsāra*)—This word is usually translated “the world of repeated births and deaths.” In this book, it is just as often used to mean “married life,” which is traditionally the focal point of the material entanglement. On the other hand, Bhaktivinoda Thakur often speaks of *kṛṣṇa-saṁsāra* in reference to the Krishna-centered householder life.

samvit (*saṁvit*)–The second of the three aspects of Krishna’s internal potency, applied to the realm of transcendental consciousness.

samadhi (*samādhi*)*–* trance. The final stage of both the *aṣṭāṅga-yoga* system and the path of remembering, though the Vaishnava concept of samadhi, when one sees Krishna’s lila in everything, is different from that of the yogis.

sambandha*—*Literally “relation.” (1) A more accurate translation would be “theology,” “dogma,” or “orthodox doctrine.” Since these words all have strong connotations related to Christianity and its history, we prefer to leave it untranslated. The word is used in a special way by Gaudiya Vaishnavas to include all aspects of metaphysics and general theology and anthropology; i.e. the nature of God, creation and man. In Gaudiya Vaishnavism, sambandha is the first of the three divisions of devotional theology, the other two being abhidheya and prayojana (q.v.). According to *Jaiva Dharma* (590), “Sambandha is the foundation of all spiritual attainment. One attains a specific final goal based on the relationship one establishes with Krishna.” (2) The first of the eleven aspects of the siddha deha.

sambandha jnana—Knowledge of relations; more specifically metaphysical knowledge of God, the world and individual souls and the relationships between them. Simplified: Krishna is God and all living beings are His servants.

*sampatti-daśā—“*stage of full possession or fulfilment.” Also called *vastu-siddhi*. *Sampatti* means possession, prosperity, welfare, good fortune, success, accomplishment, fulfilment, etc. In *Jaiva Dharma* (p. 598), *prema-sampatti-daśā.*

samskara (*saṁskāra*)—(1) The conditioned tendencies and habits accumulated through association and experience over many lifetimes. Madhusudan Saraswati compares samskaras to the imprint in sealing wax. When the mind melts due to great emotion, it accepts imprints that give it a certain form. These are called samskaras. (2) The twelve rituals or rites of passage that purify the twice born castes (*garbhādhāna, puṁsavana, sīmantonnayana, jāta-karman, nāma-karaṇa, niṣkramaṇa, anna-prāśana, cūḍā-karaṇa, upanayana, keśānta, samāvartana,* and *vivāha*).

*sanātana-dharma* = Sanatan Dharma

*sañcārī bhāva*—“Supplementary emotions.” One of the components of the rasa-experience. These are the secondary emotions that are experienced like waves in the ocean of the primary mood.

*sandhi-kāla*—Sandhi literally means the place where two things meet, so it can mean “twilight” or the morphological rules that apply when two words meet and contract in various way, or a contract or treaty between two people. Here (HNC 9.15) it refers to the limbic state in-between beginning spiritual life and perfecting it.

sandhini (*sandhinī*)*—*The first aspect of the divine energy, which is the source of transcendental being, i.e. the forms of the spiritual world.

Sankarshan (*saṅkarṣaṇa*)—the first expansion of Vasudeva in the Chaturvyuha.

*saṅketa—*as a name, signal. “when one utters Vishnu’s name but has a material concept of the Lord, or when one uses Vishnu’s name to indicate some other person or thing” (HNC 3.39-42); One of the four kinds of Namabhasa (q.v.). Other translations given are “hint” and “unintentionally.” The latter is erroneous. *Saṅketa* happens when one uses Krishna’s name to indicate something else, like another person, as happened when Ajamila named his son Narayan.

sannyas guru (*sannyāsa-guru*)*—*This guru initiates someone into the renounced order of life.

sannyasi (*sannyāsī*)—“renunciate, monk”; member of the fourth ashram (station or stage of life) in the Varnashram system, sannyas.

Sat Guru (*sad-guru*)—a genuine, qualified or bona fide spiritual teacher.

*sāyujyā—“*losing one’s individual identity and becoming one with God.” One of the five kinds of liberation, which is desired by the Mayavadis and abhorred by the Vaishnavas, who desire to always remain distinct from God in order to serve Him. (HNC 3.78ff)

seva (*sevā*)—“Service.” This is the ninth of the eleven aspects of the siddha deha. See also *bhāva-sevā.* “You are a follower of Srimati Radharani. Your service is to serve Her. If she sends you to a secluded place where you meet Krishna, and if He proposes making love to you, you should refuse His proposal. You are Radha’s maidservant, and without Her permission you cannot serve Krishna on your own. Though you love both Radha and Krishna, your love and service to Radha are more important to you than your love and service to Krishna. That is the meaning of the word seva. Serving Sri Radha through the eight periods of the day is your service.” (*Jaiva Dharma*, 592-3).

sevaparadh (*sevāparādha*)—These are the offenses made in serving the Deity form of the Lord. They are described in Chapter 14 of HNC.

Shaktyavesh (*śakty-āveśa*) avatars—*āveśa* means “possession.” Those individual souls who have been possessed by the Lord’s power and through whom He achieves His purposes are called Shaktyavesh avatars.

Shankaracharya (788-820)—The incarnation of Lord Shiva who taught the *advaita-vāda* or Mayavada philosophy.

sharanagati (*śaraṇāgati*)—The sixfold process of taking shelter (*śaraṇa*). These are described in HNC 13. Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakur placed great importance on these six processes, and wrote a book of songs called *Śaraṇāgati* in which he movingly describes the devotee’s attitude at each stage of taking shelter.

shraddha (*śraddhā*)*—*faith. The point of entry into devotional life. Divided into *pāramarthika* and *laukika* (q.v.)*.*

shringar rasa (*śṛṅgāra rasa*)—a synonym for madhura rasa (q.v.)

Shruti (*śruti*)—“that which is heard.” The original stratum of Vedic revelation is called Shruti. It most frequently refers to the Vedic mantras and the Upanishads.

shubha karma (*śubha-karma*)—“auspicious works, pious deeds.” All activities that aid the spiritual being elevate himself toward the ultimate goal of divine love for Krishna. (See chapter 11).

siddhi—success, or spiritual perfection.

siksha guru (*śikṣā guru*)*—*The spiritual teacher in more general terms. There are different kinds of instructing spiritual masters, including those who first show the aspirant the way (*vartma-pradarśaka*) and those who give more advanced instruction to one who has already been initiated and has embarked on the way (*bhajana-śikṣā-guru*).

smaran (*smaraṇa*)— “remembering, meditation.” (1) One of the nine principles activities of devotional practice. (2) One of the five modes of the *smaraṇa-daśā* (q.v.), which are described in *Bhakti-sandarbha* 278. Jiva says “even the briefest seeking out of the object of meditation.” See HNC 15.93: “Simple remembering is the stage where one recalls his spiritual identity in its eleven aspects and its relationship to his service in the circadian pastimes of the Divine Couple. At this point, there is still no constancy in such remembrance, as sometimes one remembers, while at others is distracted.”

*smaraṇa-daśā—*The third stage of *bhāva-sādhanā.* This is the essential stage where the practitioner cultivates his identification with spiritual form by meditating on it and his service to the Divine Couple in that form. According to *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta* (306): “There are five modes of remembering: *smaraṇa, dhyāna, dhāraṇā, dhruvānusmṛti* and *samādhi* \[q.v.\]*.* At the stage of remembering, one progresses from Krishna’s name to His form; at the meditational stage (*dhyāna*), at the *dhāraṇā* stage one progresses to Krishna’s qualities; at the stage of firm and constant recollection, one remembers Krishna’s pastimes. Then at the stage of trance one enters Krishna’s pastimes and is completely immersed in rasa. This is the stage of *āpana-daśā.*” Narottam Das Thakur writes in *Prema-bhakti-candrikā: smaraṇe bhābibe jāha siddha-dehe pāibe tāhā—*“You will achieve at the time of perfection whatever you have been thinking of during your meditation.” Bhaktivinoda Thakur divides remembering into the *vaidha-krama* and *rāgānuga-krama* (q.v.).

Smriti (*smṛti*)—Lit. “That which is remembered.” These are the secondary scriptures like the Puranas and lawbooks (*dharma-śāstra*).

sphurti (*sphūrti*)*—*This word should literally be translated as “hallucination” or “vision.” Other possible translations are “theophany” or “epiphany.” It refers to the sudden appearance or experience of the object of worship to the worshiper. (See HNC 15.37 fn).

*śraddhā-nāma****—*****The Holy Name chanted with faith, even by someone who is afflicted by anarthas. HNC 3.47.**

*śrāddha*—The minimum ritual duty of a Hindu man, to make offerings to the gods on behalf of departed ancestors. (not to be confused with *śraddhā*)

*śraddhābhāsa*—“a semblance of faith” HNC 3.56ff.

*śravaṇa-daśā—*The first stage of the *bhāva-mārga*, where one hears the *bhāva-tattva* from a qualified guru. In *Jaiva Dharma* (599-600), Bhaktivinoda Thakur gives a wider understanding of the hearing stage as starting with hearing about Krishna. In *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta* (305-6): “The joyful stage that comes about when one hears about the spiritual practices and their goal from a qualified teacher is called *śravaṇa-daśā*. On this stage, one hears all about how to chant the Holy Name without offenses, the process for chanting (*praṇālī*) and the qualifications to practice it.”

Sri (*śrī*)—(1) auspiciousness, wealth, beauty; (2) a name of the goddess of fortune, Lakshmi; (3) a polite title preceding men’s names; the feminine form is Srimati*. Śrīmān* and *śrīla* are other polite forms used for more respectable persons.

Sri Sampradaya—The school of Vaishnavism founded by Ramanujacharya.

*stobha—*One of the four kinds of *nāmābhāsa* (q.v.). Bhaktivinoda explains as “mocking” use of the Holy Name. Vishwanath glosses as meaningless uttering of the Holy Name, i.e., as in making sounds in a song. See HNC 3.37. Sometimes incorrectly given as *stoma.*

*śuddha*-*nāma*—The Pure Name. The Holy Name as chanted without any offenses.

suddha-sattva (*śuddha-sattva*)— “Pure goodness” or “pure being.”

*sukṛti—*Lit. “good deed.” Often translated as “piety” or “merit.” Bhaktivinoda Thakur describes different kinds of merit that lead to different results: *karmonmukhī sukṛti, jñānonmukhī sukṛti* and *bhakty-unmukhī sukṛti.* Not all kinds of pious acts lead to the devotional life.

Shunyavada (*śūnya-vāda*)*—*nihilism, or the Buddhist teaching of emptiness.

*svārasikī līlā—*Lit. “coming out of one’s personal taste.” Used as a term to refer to the “moving picture” or “flowing” pastimes of the Lord as they change over the course of the day.

*svarūpa –* Lit. “one’s own form”; essence, identity.

Svarupa Damodar—One of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu’s closest companions.

*svarūpa-lakṣaṇa—*The essential or intrinsic characteristic of a thing; an attribute that cannot in any way be separated from a thing. See HNC 4, note 4.

svarupa siddhi (*svarūpa-siddhi*)*—“*identity perfection,” i.e. full identification with the spiritual body given by the spiritual master. *Jaiva Dharma* (606): “A person who has attained the stage of *bhāvāpana-daśā* attains spiritual sight and has a vision of both his sakhi (Lalita) and his yutheshwari (Radha). Even though he may also have a vision of Lord Krishna, the master of Goloka, until his subtle material body is at last dissolved and he attains the *sampatti-daśā* (“the stage of full possession”) he does not experience Him at every moment. When he attains *bhāvāpana-daśā*, the pure soul has complete control over his gross and subtle material bodies. However, only when he attains Lord Krishna’s complete and full mercy will the soul attain the final spiritual goal, where he completely breaks off all relationship with the material world of five elements.” (HNC 15; *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta*, 317)

## —t—

*taṭastha-śakti—*marginal energy = Jiva Shakti

*taṭastha-lakṣaṇa—*The contingent or extrinsic characteristic of a thing; one that only manifests from time to time, or when it comes in contact with some other thing. See HNC 4.18. Other translations: “marginal symptoms” (ACBSP); “secondary, borderline characteristics.”

*tattva-bhrama—*The first of the four anarthas, “erroneous understanding.” It is of four kinds: of one’s own true identity, that of the supreme, of the means and end of spiritual life, and of what opposes spiritual attainment. (*Bhajana-rahasya* 2.8)

*tattva-vyavadhāna*—a “discontinuity of understanding,” i.e., between what the object is and what one thinks it is.

## \<em\>—u—\</em\>

*uddīpana—“*igniters, enkindlers.” These fall under the category of *vibhāvas*, or causes of rasa. They refer in particular to the various inspirators of love, such as Krishna’s qualities, pastimes, bodily features, or in a more general way, the scents of spring, the moonlit night, the mating cries of the koyil or chakravaka birds, etc.

*ujjvala-rasa—“*the effulgent mood.” A synonym for the *madhura-rasa* (q.v.)

*upādhi*—This term refers to false designations related to the material body that the conditioned soul identifies with. It is not used to refer to the aspects of the spiritual body.

*upāṁśu­*—“whispering.” Chanting of japa in a way that only the chanter can hear it.

*upāya*—“means” = sādhanā (HNC 1.75)

*upeya*—“end” = sādhya (HNC 1.75)

*uttama adhikārī.* The uttama adhikari is one who knowns the scriptures well, has firm faith and clear determination to practice devotional service. (*Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.2.17).

*uttama vaiṣṇava—“*the topmost or most advanced Vaishnava” (HNC chapter 4, note 21)

## —v—

*vaibhava—*derived from *vibhu*, means the qualities possessed by God (*vibhu*), or that which makes Him God. A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami primarily translates the word as “opulence,” but glories, potencies, etc., are often used. In HNC 1.15, Bhaktivinoda Thakur says *śaktira prakāśa jei, sei ta vaibhava,* “Krishna’s opulences (*vaibhava*) are the \[visible\] manifestations of His energies (*śakti*).”

*vaidha-krama—*The process leading to perfection followed by a devotee not entirely motivated by spontaneous desire.

Vaikuntha—The abode of God, especially in His majestic aspect.

*vaiṣṇavābhāsa* or *vaiṣṇava-prāya—“*almost a Vaishnava” or “not quite a Vaishnava.” Spoken a little disparagingly of the neophyte devotee. (See *kaniṣṭha-vaiṣṇava*)

vairagi (*vairāgī*)—a monk or renunciate.

vairagya (*vairāgya*)—renunciation. The renounced order.

*vāmya—*The character traits of a woman who dominates her lover, who is jealous and possessive (known as the *vāmā nāyikā*). Opposite of *dākṣiṇya.*

*varaṇa-daśā*—“The accepting stage.” The second stage of *bhāva-sādhana,* when after hearing about the spiritual body, one accepts it. See *Jaiva Dharma* (600-601). In *Caitanya-śikṣāmṛta* (306): “When one has become worthy, he receives a japa mala that has been imbued with the Holy Name and ecstatic love from the spiritual master; in other words, the stage where the disciple with great satisfaction accepts the pure method of chanting from the spiritual master and is empowered by him to follow it is called *varaṇa-daśā.*”

*varṇa-vyavadhāna—*a discontinuity in the syllables of the Holy Name. When these syllables are separated by other sounds.

*vāsa-sthāna—*“place of residence.” One of the eleven aspects of the siddha form. It usually refers to a particular kunj by Radha Kund, such as Svananda-sukhada Kunj in the case of Bhaktivinoda Thakur himself. In *Jaiva Dharma*, p. 592, Goswami says, “Eternal residence in Vraja is called *vāsa*. You will take birth as a certain gopi in a certain village within the land of Vraja and be married to a certain gopa in another village. Nevertheless, you will be attracted by the sound of Krishna’s flute and go off to follow one of the sakhis to Radha Kund, where you will have a cottage in her personal forest grove. The residence which is established by your spiritual ego-consciousness is your eternal residence.”

*vastu-siddhi—“*concrete perfection” (Cś 318) After the material body has passed away while one is at the stage of *āpana-daśā.* Also called *sampatti-daśā.* The expression *sampatti-siddhi* is also found in *Jaiva Dharma.* Ref. to *Bhajana-rahasya*S 1.3.29 and 1.4.12.

*vayas—*age. The second of the eleven *bhāvas* or aspects of the *siddha-deha*. In Jaiva Dharma (p. 591), Gopal Guru Goswami says, “As your relationship with Krishna awakens, a wonderful spiritual form also manifests. In your case it is that of a beautiful girl in Vraja, which will be of an age appropriate to your service. This means adolescence or *kaiśora*, which is the age between ten and sixteen. It is also known as *vayaḥ-sandhi*, or the juncture between childhood and adulthood. Thus as you advance in service, your age will increase from ten up to sixteen. Infancy, childhood and old age do not exist for the beautiful girls of Vraja. Therefore you should think of yourself in a spiritual form in the *kaiśora* age.”

*veṣa*—apparel, clothing. One of the eleven *bhāvas* or aspects of the *siddha-deha*.

*vibhāva*—opulence.

*vibhu*—A secondary name of God, refering to His divine powers or opulences.

*vikṣepa*—distraction. See HNC 12.

*vilāsa-vigraha—*A class of the Lord’s unlimited forms; all of which have four arms like Narayan (HNV 5, note 1).

Vishnu tattva (*viṣṇu-tattva*)—the Godhead. The Godhead includes all the different personal manifestations of the Supreme Lord as well as the direct expressions of His internal potency.

Vishnuswami—of uncertain date. The founder of one of the four Vaishnava sampradayas.

*vivarta-vāda—*The Mayavada doctrine that holds that Brahman itself is transformed into the universe.

*vyavadhāna­—“*discontinuity.” Also translated as “separation, disruption.” There are two kinds, described in chapter two. See *varṇa-vyavadhāna* and *tattva-vyavadhāna.*

## \<em\>—y—\</em\>

*yoga-pīṭha—*Lit. *“*The seat of union.” The place where Radha and Krishna meet in Vrindavan and are united with all their friends. The site of the Govindaji temple in Vrindavan.

*yogyatā—*qualification. This is used also in the sense of “applicable.”

*yogya-smaraṇa.* The kind of meditation that is applicable to one.

yuga—The ages, named Satya, Treta, Dvapara, and Kali.

*yukta-vairāgya—“*appropriate or engaged detachment”

*yukti—*rationale, reason, argument

*yūtha—*“group, cohort.” See yutheshwari. This is one of the eleven aspects of the spiritual identity.

yutheshwari (*yūtheśvarī*)*—*The different gopis who compete for Krishna’s affection, each of whom have a *yūtha*, or coterie of sakhis. Of all the yutheshwaris, Radharani is the most important. Lalita, Visakha and the other sakhis belong to her group, even though they have the qualities that make them capable of acting as competitors for Krishna’s love.
